It is opened against the main branch, titled `[S-nnnn] <title>`, with the body `forge.StoryPullRequest.PullRequestBody` builds: the project's `.github/pull_request_template.md` with the places it marks filled. `<!-- flai:story -->` to `<!-- /flai:story -->` gets the story's ID; `criteria`, its acceptance criteria ticked as the story has them; `verify`, its last `flai verify` result and commit; `definition-of-done` is kept as the project wrote it. A part the template does not mark is appended under its own heading (`Story`, `Acceptance criteria`, `Definition of done`, `Last flai verify`), so a project without the file gets all four. A begin mark without its end mark, or the reverse, is refused. An open pull request whose title, body, or base differs is updated; otherwise it is left `unchanged`.
- **What it keeps.** `.flai-cache/forge/S-nnnn.json` (`serve.ForgeState`): the outcome (`pushed`, `up-to-date`, `failed`, or `skipped`) and its reason, the local and remote heads, the head flai last pushed, which the next lease names, when it pushed and whether with the lease, the pull request's number and URL and what was done to it, and the failure thread. It never holds the token.
- **Failures.** A push or a request that fails, such as an unset token, a refused push, or the forge being down, opens one thread on the story by `flai serve`, titled `flai serve could not push S-nnnn's branch or open its pull request`, with the reason, which says what clears it. A changed reason adds an entry; the same reason adds none. The next look tries again, and the first that succeeds resolves the thread. A story that leaves review has its thread resolved too. A thread someone resolved while the same failure stands is not opened again until the reason changes. The journal records a push or a request that changed something, and a failure the first time its reason is seen.
- **The token is kept from agents.** Every agent `flai serve` starts, story agents and their sub-agents and the strategic agents, runs without any forge token's variable, whatever the project's mode: each forge's default, such as `GITHUB_TOKEN`, and any `flai serve forge` names (`launcher.withoutForgeTokens`, beside `withoutSpendKeys`). `flai guard` refuses `flai forge push` to every agent.

`flai serve checks` names commands the host runs on a story in review. They are not the forge's checks, which the pass reads back ([below](#reading-the-pull-request-back)).

### Reading the pull request back

S-0394, [ADR-0167](../adrs/0167-flai-serve-reads-each-pull-request-s-required-checks-and-reviews-into-kept.md). When the push of a story in review ends `pushed` or `up-to-date` with a pull request, the same look reads the pull request back through the forge contract (`internal/serve/forgestatus.go`): `FindPullRequest` by its head branch, `Checks` on its head commit, of which it keeps the required ones, `Reviews`, and `RequiredApprovals` on its base.

- **What it keeps.** `.flai-cache/forge/status/S-nnnn.json` (`forge.Status`, `internal/forge/state.go`), one file per story, replaced whole at each read. It never holds the token.

  | Field | Holds |
  |-------|-------|
  | `pull_request`, `url`, `head_sha`, `read_at` | The pull request's number and page, the head commit it was read at, and when |
  | `state` | Whether the pull request was `open`, `closed`, or `merged` when read (S-0397); empty in a file kept before flai recorded it |
  | `checks` | Each required check, in the forge's order: `name`, `state` (`pending`, `passed`, `failed`), `url` (its log), `summary`, and `since`, when it entered that state |
  | `reviews` | Each review, oldest first: `author`, `state` (`approved`, `changes-requested`, `commented`, `dismissed`, `pending`), `url`, `body`, `submitted_at` |
  | `required_approvals` | The approving reviews the base branch requires, as last read (S-0395); 0 when it requires none or the token cannot read its rules |
  | `events` | Appended, never rewritten: `head` for a new head commit, `check` for a required check that appeared or changed state, `review` for a review that is new or changed, each with `at`, `name`, `from`, `to`, and the `commit` it was seen at. S-0396 reads them |
  | `sent_back` | Each failure flai sent the story back for: `key`, `kind` (`check` or `review`), `name`, `commit`, `url`, `thread`, `at` |

- **A read that fails.** The forge not answering, or no open pull request found, is logged once per look (`pull requests in review not read`), opens no thread, and leaves the kept file as it was. The next look reads again.
- **Who reads it.** `flai show` and its `--json`, the MCP `item_get`, `flai forge status`, and the host read method `forge.status` ([flaiover-dashboard.md](flaiover-dashboard.md)). Each reads the file, leaves `events` out, and never calls the forge, so an agent without the token sees the same state and no reader spends the forge's rate limit.
- **The send back.** A required check `failed` at the head commit sends the story back. So does a reviewer whose latest review that approves, requests changes, or was dismissed requests changes, as the forge counts a reviewer's decision (`Status.Decisions`, which [the acceptance gate](#the-acceptance-gate) counts by too). A pending check does nothing. flai serve opens one thread on the story, by `flai serve`, for everything new at that read, titled `Pull request #<n> of S-nnnn: <what failed>`, such as `the required check ci failed` or `alex requested changes`. The thread names each check and links its log, or names the reviewer and links the review, and quotes the check's summary or the review's comment up to 500 characters; when the markdown lint refuses the quote, it goes in a code block. Then flai moves the story from review to in-progress, by `flai`, with a reason naming each failure, such as `the required check ci failed on 3f2a9c1d0b7e of pull request #12`, and journals the move under the action `forge`.
- **Once per failure.** A failed check is keyed by its name and the head commit (`check:<name>@<commit>`), so the same check failing on a new commit sends the story back again. A review is keyed by its URL (`review:<url>`), so a review that requests changes sends the story back once, across the fix's commits, until the reviewer reviews again. When the thread opened and the move failed, the next look moves the story without a new thread.
- **Resolved.** flai serve resolves the thread once the story is back in review at another head commit, and once the story is done or cancelled, archived or not. A thread someone else resolved stays resolved.
- **The agent.** flai serve starts the story's agent for the thread, spending no automatic restart; the `flai serve agent` row in [Commands](#commands) says when. The agent fixes the branch, closes out, and moves the story to review, and the next look pushes the fix.

### The acceptance gate

S-0395, [ADR-0169](../adrs/0169-under-pull-request-acceptance-every-acceptance-the-operator-s-and-the.md). Under `acceptance.mode: pull-request`, the preview of a story's acceptance (`forgeGate` and `preview.ForgeBlockers` in `internal/preview/accept.go`) reads the kept `forge/status/S-nnnn.json` and judges it against the story branch's head. It needs no token and never calls the forge. Each blocker is a `preview.Blocker` `{code, message, name?, url?}` in `--json`'s `forge_blockers`, and its message is in `blockers`:

| Code | When | `name`, `url` |
|------|------|---------------|
| `pull_request_missing` | No kept state, or a kept file that cannot be read | none |
| `forge_stale` | The state was read at another commit than the branch head, or the head cannot be read; listed alone | the pull request |
| `check_failing` | A required check failed; one per check, with its summary | the check, its log |
| `check_pending` | A required check has not finished; one per check | the check, its log |
| `changes_requested` | A reviewer's decision (`Status.Decisions`) requests changes; one per reviewer | the reviewer, the review |
| `approvals_missing` | Fewer decisions approve than `required_approvals` | the pull request |

The preview's JSON also has `pull_request`, the kept pull request's URL. `flai accept` refuses on these blockers before anything is merged, as on any blocker. They apply whoever accepts: the operator, the dashboard through the host write `accept.run`, and the orchestrator with `--by orchestrator`, beside its own blockers ([strategic-agents.md](strategic-agents.md#accepting-a-story-s-0221)). An acceptance that resumes at the commit step is not gated again. Under `local` the preview is unchanged. A pull request kept as merged passes the gate, whatever its checks and reviews, since the forge merged it ([below](#the-merge-through-the-remote)).

### The merge through the remote

S-0397, [ADR-0170](../adrs/0170-under-pull-request-acceptance-flai-accept-merges-the-story-s-pull-request-on.md). Under `acceptance.mode: pull-request`, once the gate passes, `flai accept` merges no branch on this machine (`acceptThroughRemote` in `cmd/accept_remote.go`). In place of the local rebase and fast-forward it:

1. Refuses, before anything changes, a story worktree with uncommitted changes, a project with no `origin`, and a local main with commits not on `origin`'s main that change paths outside `wip/`, naming each commit. Code no pull request carried would reach `origin`'s main past the review at the next publish.
2. Merges the story's pull request on the forge with `acceptance.merge_method`, at the head the gate passed at, and deletes `story/S-nnnn` on `origin` with the host's git credentials (`serve.MergeStory`, `internal/serve/forgemerge.go`). A delete that fails is reported, and the merge stands.
3. Fetches `origin`'s main and checks that the merge commit is on it. It rebases local main's unpublished `wip/` commits onto it in a temporary detached worktree under `.flai-cache`, then moves main there with `git reset --keep`, so the main checkout's uncommitted edits survive.
4. Removes the story's worktree, and its branch with `git branch -D`, since a squash or rebase merge leaves it unmerged in git's eyes.

Then it moves the story to done, archives it, and commits, as under `local`. The acceptance commit's subject and the archive are the same in both modes.

- **Who merges.** Where the token's variable can be read, `flai accept` calls `MergeStory` in its own process. Where it cannot, as for every agent, it hands the merge to flai serve: it writes `.flai-cache/forge/merge/S-nnnn.request.json` with the story, the head, the method, who asks, and when. flai serve's merge pass looks every second, renames a request to `S-nnnn.taken.json`, merges with its own token, writes `S-nnnn.answer.json` (`serve.MergeAnswer`: outcome `merged`, `already-merged`, or `refused`, the reason, the merge commit, and whether the branch was deleted), and journals it under the `forge` action as `serve.merge`. `flai accept` waits 30 s for the take and 5 min for the answer, and refuses when either runs out. An interrupt withdraws a request not yet taken. The hand-off adds no listener and no secret.
- **The host action.** The merge is behind the `forge` host action. A merge made in `flai accept`'s own process is refused while it is off, naming `flai serve enable forge`. A handed-off merge is answered refused by flai serve, by its own configuration. The host write `accept.run` asks for the `forge` action under `pull-request` (`forgeAcceptance` in `internal/hostapi/writes.go`), so the dashboard's acceptance is refused and journalled while it is off.
- **The dashboard.** `accept.run` runs `flai accept` in flai serve's environment, with `FLAI_AGENT=flaiover`, where the token is at hand, so it merges directly and never hands off. Without the token there it is refused, naming the variable: flai serve has none to merge with either.
- **Refusals.** A merge the forge refuses, for a conflict, a branch out of date, or a rule, stops the acceptance before anything changes locally, naming the forge's reason (`ErrMergeRefused`). So do a pull request closed without merging, one whose head moved since the gate read it, and a story with no pull request. A step that fails after the forge's merge says so and leaves the request merged; running `flai accept` again finds it merged and goes on.
- **Already merged.** A pull request someone merged on the forge, a reviewer, say, is found by its number (`Forge.PullRequest`) and accepted with no second merge: `flai accept` fetches and goes on from step 3. Its remote branch is deleted as after a merge.
- **A merged request stays merged.** The kept status records `state` (`forge.Status.State`). flai serve's forge pass never pushes the branch of a story whose pull request is merged and opens no other pull request for it (outcome `merged`). Its read-back sends nothing back on it.
- **The dry run.** It prints `would merge pull request #<n> through the remote by <method>[ through flai serve], then fetch main and bring the main branch up to origin's, and remove story/S-nnnn and its worktree`, or `pull request #<n> is merged on the forge already: would accept S-nnnn with no second merge, …`.
- **`--json`.** The result carries `remote` (`preview.RemoteMerge`): `pull_request`, `head`, `method`, `already_merged`, `through_serve`, and, after the real run, `outcome`, `sha`, `branch_deleted`, and `branch_reason`.
- **Publishing.** The story's code reaches `origin`'s main at the merge. The acceptance commit and the archive reach it only when published (`flai release --pending`). A main branch that refuses every direct push refuses that publish too; that is open (TH-0464).

### The merge queue pass

S-0399, [ADR-0173](../adrs/0173-a-merge-queue-in-flai-serve-with-no-model-runs-behind-the-merge-queue-host.md). `flai serve` runs one merge queue pass per served project (`queuePass` in `internal/serve/mergequeue.go`), with no model. It looks at start, every 30 seconds (`queueEvery`), and at once when the project's work items or threads change. It runs a batch only while all of these hold, and otherwise logs `merge queue not run` once with a `reason`, until the reason changes:

| Holds | `reason` when it does not |
|-------|---------------------------|
| The `merge-queue` host action is on for the project | none: nothing is logged |
| The project and its manifest can be read | `unreadable`, a warning |
| `orchestration.permissions.accept_reviews` is on | `accept_reviews_off` |
| `acceptance.mode` is `local` | `pull_request` |
| The state can be read | `unreadable`, a warning |
| The queue is not held | `held`, with `held_by` |

A queue that may run again after one of these is logged as `merge queue may run again`. One batch runs per project at a time (`queueRuns`), in a goroutine of the pass, so that no agent's turn and no request waits on its tiers.

- **What it picks.** `mergequeue.RunBatch` (`internal/mergequeue/batch.go`) does nothing under pull-request acceptance, while the state's batch has a result and is not dropped, which waits to be landed or bisected, or when no story is to be batched. Otherwise it takes the enqueued stories that `Compute` lists as eligible, in the order they were enqueued, at most `Manifest.BatchMax()`: `merge_queue.batch_max`, 4 when unset, validated by `MergeQueue.Errors` and written by `flai manifest set` ([settings](../../docs/operators/settings.md#project-manifest)). It passes over a story whose last outcome is `left_out` or `waiting` at the main branch's commit and the head the story has now. A batch with no result and not dropped, as a `flai serve` stopped mid-run leaves it, and a dropped one are replaced.
- **The build.** It removes the last batch's worktree and branch, then adds the worktree `.flai-cache/worktrees/merge-queue` (`WorktreeName`) on the branch `merge-queue/<id>` (`BranchPrefix`, the ID the start's UTC time, such as `20261019-100600`) from the main branch's head. It rebases a copy of each story's commits beyond its merge base with the main branch, up to the head its `flai verify` passed at, onto the batch in turn, with `rebase.updateRefs` off, and moves the batch's branch to the result. The stories' own branches and worktrees are not touched. A rebase that stops is continued over the stops `flai stream sync` settles, with the same `storygit.ContinueOverResolved` and the files `issues.FilesIn` binds to the batch's worktree: the issue files merged by their instances, the ADR index row by row, the shared markdown files by sections, and `summary.md` written again ([ADR-0175](../adrs/0175-a-rebase-that-stops-on-a-markdown-file-inside-the-manifest-s-shared-paths.md), the `flai stream sync` row above). A story whose every stop they settle stays in the batch, and the log says what was merged and written again. The fold after a clean rebase is not run: the landing's acceptance runs it on the story's own branch. A stop they do not settle is aborted and the story taken out of the batch: its conflicting paths go on the batch's `left_out`, and its outcome, with the base and its head, goes by `classify` over the paths `Files.Unresolved` leaves, those the steps do not resolve, a shared markdown file among them:

  | Those paths | Outcome | What the batch does |
  |-------------|---------|---------------------|
  | All inside `claims.shared` | `waiting` | Keeps it enqueued; the detail names the paths and says it is never sent back for them. The next run passes over it until the base or its head moves. |
  | Any outside, among the paths a story kept ahead of it changed on the batch | `left_out` | Keeps it enqueued, passed over likewise, and messages it from each such story ahead, by `storygit.ConflictAuthor`, in the pair's conversation, `about` the paths both change (`messages.Notify`, as `tellConflicts` does, [ADR-0121](../adrs/0121-flai-tells-two-stories-of-a-trial-merge-conflict-or-a-grown-overlap-with-a.md)). A message that cannot be sent is logged. |
  | Any outside, changed by no story ahead | `sent_back` | Moves it back to `in-progress` as `flai`, with the paths and `flai stream sync` as the reason, and dequeues it. A move that fails is logged and the story is left out instead. |

  A rebase git failed without stopping, or a stop on no path, is `left_out`. With every story taken out, the batch is removed and the state left with none.
- **The verification.** `CheckoutVerifier` reads the tiers from the batch worktree's `system-flow.yaml` for this flai (`verify.CheckoutTiersFor`) and runs `verify.Tiers` there: the tiers `SelectStory` selects for the paths the batch changes against the main branch, cheapest first, stopping at the first that fails. It leaves no tier out: those marked `queue`, which `flai verify` leaves to the batch while the queue can batch, run here whenever the batch's paths select them ([ADR-0174](../adrs/0174-while-the-merge-queue-can-batch-flai-verify-and-the-close-out-leave-the-test.md)). It runs in `flai serve`'s process, reuses no earlier run, sets no `CLOSE_OUT_STORY`, and stores no report, so a story's own `flai verify` result is unchanged. It runs each tier with `MERGE_QUEUE_STORIES` set to the stories the batch kept, comma-separated in batch order, and each bisection step with the stories that step verifies, so that `scripts/check.sh`, `scripts/lint-md.sh`, and the integration checks scope to them as a close-out's scope to its story, recording no issue: a finding on main outside every one of them fails no batch (S-0464, [ADR-0183](../adrs/0183-the-merge-queue-s-batch-and-each-bisection-step-run-their-tiers-with-merge.md), I-0174). A batch dropped meanwhile keeps its result and stays dropped.
- **The logs.** S-0457. The verification sets `verify.RunOptions.LogDir`, so each tier that runs writes its whole output to `<tier>.log`, the tier's name with each character outside `A-Z a-z 0-9 . _ -` made `-`, in the batch's folder `.flai-cache/merge-queue/batches/<id>/` (`BatchLogs`), beside `state.json`, where it outlives the batch's worktree; each step of a bisection writes to `step-<n>/` below it, for the n-th step, emptied first when that step is verified again. When a batch is built, `pruneLogs` keeps the newest 20 batch folders (`logsKept`), its own among them, by batch ID, which is when each was built, and removes the older ones; a prune that fails is logged at warn, `merge queue batch logs not pruned; the batch goes on`, and the batch goes on. `flai verify`, `flai test`, and the close-out set no `LogDir` and write no logs.
- **The state.** Each step is written to the batch in `state.json` through `Update` as it begins: `building`, `rebasing S-nnnn`, `verifying`, then `verifying <tier>` as each tier starts, and `verified` or `failed`. The result is `{passed, stopped_at, summary, findings, finished_at, log}`: `stopped_at` the failing tier, or `verify` when the tiers could not run; `summary` the tiers that passed, or the failing tier and its first three findings, at most 600 characters; `findings`, since S-0457, the failing tier's findings as `flai verify` prints them under the step (`verify.StepFindings`), every line, a plain tier's failure lines and the tail of its output among them, cut at 8,000 characters (`findingsMax`) at a line's end and marked `… N more characters left out; the tier's whole output is in its log`, and empty when the batch passed, its tiers could not run, or the failing tier had none; and `log` the folder of its tiers' logs, relative to the project's root (above), empty when nothing was written there. `flai queue`, `flai board`, the MCP `board`, `board.get`, and the dashboard read it.
- **The hold.** A batch already running finishes when the queue is held, and is taken on (below) in the same look; the next look sees the hold, and neither starts a batch nor takes one on.
- **Stopping.** When `flai serve` stops, or stops serving the project, the batch running is cancelled and waited for. It writes no result, and its worktree is left for the next run to build again.
S-0400, [ADR-0176](../adrs/0176-the-merge-queue-lands-each-story-of-a-passing-batch-through-flai-accept-batch.md). After the batch's turn, in the same goroutine and under the same claim, `advanceBatch` takes a verified batch on with `mergequeue.Advance` (`internal/mergequeue/land.go`). It does nothing under pull-request acceptance, with no batch, or with one dropped or not verified.

- **Main moving.** `DropIfMainMoved` drops, as `flai serve`, a verified batch whose main branch is not where the batch expects it: the landing's `head`, or the batch's base before any landing. The reason is `the main branch moved from <a> to <b> while merge queue batch <id> waited to land; it is built again from the new main`. `Advance` checks first, and `Land` before each landing.
- **Landing.** For a batch that passed, `Advance` writes `landing` with every story, `landed` empty, and `head` the base. `Land` lands the next story, one at a time: it writes the step `landing <story>`, accepts the story as whoever enqueued it, and records main's new head as the landing's `head`. `flaiAccepter` (`internal/serve/mergequeue.go`) accepts by running `flai accept <story> --by=<by> --batch=<id>` with this flai's own executable in the main checkout, with `FLAI_AGENT=flai-serve`, no `FLAI_SESSION`, and none of the variables that mark an agent's run. A story no longer enqueued drops the batch. Since S-0402 ([ADR-0178](../adrs/0178-the-merge-queue-lands-each-story-in-the-main-checkout-with-git-s-own-check.md)) `Land` checks each story just before it lands it (`overwrittenBy`, `storygit.Overwritten`): a story whose change would overwrite an uncommitted or untracked file in the main checkout, one that became dirty after the batch was built, is held back by `holdBack`, which records `{story, paths}` in the landing's `held_back` and the outcome `held_back` with `HeldBackText`, keeps the story enqueued, and logs a warn, `merge queue story held back; its change would overwrite uncommitted files in the main checkout`, with `batch`, `story`, `paths`, and `free`, the `FreeCommand`; the rest land in queue order, and eligibility's `dirty_main` keeps the story out of the next batch until the files are clean. A landing that fails drops the batch with `landing <story> failed: <its last five lines on standard error>`, so the stories not landed are batched again from main. Once every story the landing names has landed or is held back, the batch's worktree and branch are removed and the state left with no batch, so `RunBatch` batches the stories still enqueued.
- **Bisection.** For a batch that failed, `RunBisect` (`internal/mergequeue/bisect.go`) starts with `good` 0, main itself, and `bad` the whole batch. Each step verifies main with the first `(good+bad)/2` stories: it checks out, detached, the last one's rebased head in the batch's worktree and runs the batch's verification. A pass raises `good`, a failure lowers `bad`, until the two are one apart. Then `failing` is the story at `bad`, `commit` its rebased head, `result` the failing step's result, or the batch's own, and `landing` the stories before it. The step becomes `bisected`, and the batch's branch is checked out again. Each step is written as it begins and once it has a result. A step that a stopped run left without a result is verified again in place. Heads that do not match the stories one to one, or a bisection that cannot run, drop the batch.
- **Send-back.** `SendBack` opens a thread on the failing story by `flai serve`, `Merge queue batch <id> fails with <story> at <tier>`, saying what failed, at which commit, with which stories, and what to do; an open one of that title is reused. What failed is quoted in a fenced block (`failingText`): since S-0457, the failing step's `findings` in full, or its `summary` when the result has none, as one kept by an older flai or one whose tiers could not run, followed by `The whole output of each tier it ran is in <log>.` when the result has a log folder. It then moves the story from review to in-progress by `flai`, with the reason `merge queue batch <id> fails with <story> at <commit>: <summary>`, which keeps the one-line summary. A story no longer in review is neither moved nor given a thread. Either way the story leaves the queue with the outcome `failing`, and the bisection's `sent_back` is set. The stories before it then land as above. The agent launcher's `startQueueSentBack` (`internal/serve/agents.go`) starts the story's agent again as `startSentBack` does for the forge: once, while the story is in progress, not blocked, the thread open, and its last run ended before the send-back and was not stopped; in that run's session when it had one; told the thread and what failed (`harness.SentBack.Queue`). At each look, before the batch's turn, `ResolveSentBack` resolves that thread as `flai serve` once the story is in review again at a head other than the one that failed, so it can be enqueued and batched again.
- **The state of the end.** The batch keeps `heads`, `{story, verified, rebased}` per story in the order rebased, written as each rebase succeeds; `bisect`, `{steps, good, bad, failing, commit, result, sent_back}`, each step `{stories, commit, started_at, result}`; and `landing`, `{stories, landed, head}`, with `held_back`, `{story, paths}` per story held back, since S-0402. The steps add `bisecting <k> of <n>`, then `bisecting <k> of <n>, verifying <tier>` as each tier starts, `bisected`, and `landing <story>`. The outcomes add `landed` and `failing`, and since S-0402 `held_back`.
- **What it logs.** One line a look: `merge queue batch advanced` with `batch`, `landed`, `sent_back`, and `took`; `merge queue batch dropped` with the reason; or `merge queue batch not advanced` with the error. The host's journal keeps each story landed or sent back, and each batch dropped, under the action `merge-queue`, by `flai serve`. When the advance did something and nothing failed, the pass looks again at once.
- **The event log.** S-0404, [ADR-0177](../adrs/0177-flai-stats-reports-per-week-the-stories-the-merge-queue-landed-or-sent-back.md). `mergequeue.Update`, the state's only writer, compares the state before and after each write and appends a line per event it made (`enqueued`, `batch_built`, `verified`, `bisected`, `landed`, `sent_back`, `dequeued`) to `.flai-cache/merge-queue/events.jsonl` beside `state.json` (`internal/mergequeue/log.go`), so `flai serve`, `flai queue add` and `remove`, the host API, and `flai accept` are all covered; `flai stats` reads it ([metrics.md](metrics.md#merge-queue-s-0404)). It is append-only and never trimmed. An append that fails is logged at warn, `merge queue events not logged`, and the write stands.

## Versions: the host's flai and the tree

The flai installed on the host serves MCP and starts agents, and it lags the tree it serves whenever a release is accepted and not installed (S-0181, I-0049, I-0051). These keep that from going unnoticed or breaking the board:

- **It reads past what it does not know.** Work items, threads, and issues are decoded leniently on the paths that list and serve them; an unknown front-matter key is kept, written back unchanged, logged once per file, and reported by `flai check` ([work-hierarchy.md](work-hierarchy.md#fields-an-older-flai-does-not-know)).
- **It says when it is behind.** `release.FlaiOutdated(runner, root, running)` finds the newest `flai/vX.Y.Z` tag reachable from the project's HEAD (`git tag --merged HEAD --list 'flai/v*'`) and, when the running flai is a release below it, answers `Outdated{running, newest, upgrade, message}`; a dev build, a project with no such tag, and a git that cannot say answer nothing. An answer is kept a minute per project and running version. `flai serve` logs `flai is older than the project it serves` (warn) when it starts serving such a project, and checks again every minute, as the next bullet but one says; the MCP `inbox` carries `flai_outdated` on every call while it is true, and the server's instructions tell the agent to tell the designer; `project.info` carries `flai_outdated`, and the dashboard's host badge then reads `host flai: upgrade to X.Y.Z`, its title naming both versions and the upgrade. The upgrade is `buildinfo.UpgradeCommand`: `flai host upgrade`, or `flai self-upgrade` where no flai host runs.
- **It closes no task by rules a release raised.** `flai task done`, the MCP tool `task_done`, and the host channel's `task.done` refuse, changing nothing, while `FlaiOutdated` answers and the newest tag raised task done's rules or the major version (S-0363, [ADR-0142](../adrs/0142-flai-task-done-refuses-to-close-a-task-while-the-running-flai-s-major-or-minor.md), I-0127; S-0445, [ADR-0180](../adrs/0180-flai-task-done-refuses-an-older-flai-only-when-the-newest-flai-release-raised.md), I-0157). The rules are a number: `flai/internal/buildinfo/task-done-rules.txt` (`buildinfo.TaskDoneRulesPath`) holds one positive integer, embedded as `buildinfo.TaskDoneRules`, and `flai version --json` prints it as `task_done_rules`. A tag records it as that file at the commit it tags; nothing is written into the tag. `FlaiOutdated` reads it with `git show <tag>:<path>` into `Outdated.NewestRules`, 0 when that commit has no such file. `taskdone.BehindRules` refuses when the tag's number is above the running flai's (`rules`), or its major version is above (`major`). When either has no number, ADR-0142's rule holds (`BehindRelease`): a major or minor version behind refuses. The check runs at the start of `taskdone.Run`, before the commit step. The refusal names the running and newest versions, both numbers when the rules were raised, and the upgrade command. A patch or minor release that keeps the number closes the task, and the inbox carries `flai_outdated`. Every other command only warns. Moving the file makes every later tag fall back to the minor rule.
- **An outdated MCP server closes with the installed flai.** A story's MCP server is a stdio child of its agent's session and keeps its binary until the session ends, so after a host upgrade it still runs the old flai (I-0147). When the check above would refuse, the MCP tool `task_done` reads the version and `task_done_rules` of the binary at the path the server recorded when it started (`os.Executable`), with `<path> version --json`. When that flai has caught up, its number not below the newest tag's and its major version not below the tag's, or, when either has no number, its major and minor not below the tag's (ADR-0180), it runs `<path> task done <task> --json` with `-m` and `--log` as given, in the main checkout, with `FLAI_AGENT` and `FLAI_SESSION` set to the server's, and answers the result it prints, a stop included, with `closed_by` naming that flai's version and path. Otherwise it refuses as above, naming the installed flai's version, or why it could not be read, and telling the agent to call again once the host is upgraded (S-0390, [ADR-0160](../adrs/0160-an-outdated-mcp-server-s-task-done-closes-the-task-with-the-installed-flai-once.md)).
- **It catches up, or asks.** On its minute loop `flai serve` calls `FlaiOutdated` for each served project (S-0387, [ADR-0158](../adrs/0158-flai-serve-checks-each-served-project-for-a-newer-flai-release-every-minute.md), I-0141). With the shell-only host action `auto-upgrade` on for a project that is behind, it asks the host for `POST /upgrade {version}` with that project's newest tag, the highest where several are behind, as `flai host upgrade --version` does, journals it, and logs `flai upgrade asked` (info). The host verifies, installs, and restarts as for the operator's upgrade. Detached agents, story and strategic, run on, and the new `flai serve` adopts them. No upgrade is asked while a dashboard write runs in `flai serve`'s process, such as an acceptance, which a restart would cut. A refused upgrade, such as a release tagged but not yet published, is logged (warn) and asked again no sooner than 15 minutes later; an hour after the first ask, still not installed, it opens the thread below with the last refusal. With `auto-upgrade` off, `flai serve` opens one thread on the project's `system-flow.yaml`, as `flai serve`, naming both versions and asking the operator to run `flai host upgrade` or turn on `auto-upgrade`: one per project and newest release, recorded under the serve directory so neither the loop nor a restart opens it twice. The thread waits for the release to be published ([ADR-0161](../adrs/0161-flai-serve-s-upgrade-thread-names-a-flai-release-only-once-it-is-published-and.md), I-0149): before it opens it, `flai serve` looks for the release among the published ones, as `flai self-upgrade --list` reads them. While it is not there, it logs `flai upgrade thread waits for the release to be published` (info), opens and records nothing, and looks again on the next minute's loop; a release found published is not looked for again, and when the releases cannot be listed it opens the thread as before and logs the failure (warn).
- **A tier refusal names the version.** When the manifest's test tiers do not validate and `FlaiOutdated` answers, `flai test`, `flai verify`, and the MCP tools `test` and `verify` say that the running flai is older than the newest release in the project's history, so the manifest may use what it does not know, and name the upgrade command, rather than only telling the agent to fix the manifest (ADR-0158, I-0141: flai 1.40.1 refused `{importers}`).
- **It stops below the project's minimum.** `flai.minimum` in `system-flow.yaml` names the oldest flai release that may read the project; `manifest.Load` refuses a flai below it with `manifest.TooOldError` before any item is read, so commands, `flai serve` (the project is unavailable, with the reason), and `flai mcp` (folder mode logs `project not served` once per reason) stop and name the version needed ([project-manifest.md](project-manifest.md)).
- **The minimum rises no higher than the flai that publishes.** `flai release --pending` (and `flai push --pending` with `auto-publish`, the operator's shell tool) raise it in the publish commit (`release.RaiseMinimum`, I-0107, S-0321). A release is a candidate when its `flai/internal/workitem/front-matter-fields.txt`, the list of fields flai reads that `TestFieldsFileIsTheCode` keeps equal to the code, differs from the previous `flai/v*` release's, that release having had one. The release being published is a candidate when HEAD's file differs from its last tag's. The minimum becomes the newest candidate above it that is no newer than the flai running the publish, so it only ever names a release whose binaries exist. A newer candidate waits: the first publish from a flai at or above it raises the minimum to it. So the release that adds a front-matter field becomes the minimum at the first publish after the host's flai is upgraded to it. A flai that is not a release build (`dev`, a snapshot) raises nothing. The minimum never falls. A first release, and a fields file the previous release did not have (the release of S-0181 itself), raise nothing. Any change makes a candidate, a removed field too, which is conservative. A git that cannot say is an error, and so is a clone without the last release's tag. The publish logs what it did: `flai.minimum raised` (info, with the upgrade command), `flai.minimum raise waits` (info: a publish from flai X.Y.Z or newer raises it), or `flai.minimum not raised` (warn: the flai publishing is not a release build). Before S-0321 a publish wrote the release being cut as the minimum before its binaries were built, so the host's `flai serve` stopped loading the project and killed the publish that raised it.
- **A served project that stops loading drains.** When a registered project's manifest stops loading while `flai serve` serves it, a raised minimum say, `reconcile` stops its looks and watcher at once and its client refuses every new request (`channel.Client.Drain`) with `CodeUnknownProject` and `flai no longer serves project "<key>": <reason>`. Requests in flight answer for at most `serve.Options.DrainGrace` (`DefaultDrainGrace`, 5 minutes); then the client stops. `project dropped` is logged once, after the drain, with the reason and the number of requests left unanswered, at warn when any were. The project is not served again until its drain ends. A project removed from the registry, or served under another key, still stops at once, and so does one removed while it drains (S-0321).

Version comparison is `buildinfo.Semver` and `buildinfo.Below`: `X.Y.Z` with an optional `v`, a pre-release or build suffix ignored; anything else is not a release and is never below or above anything.

### Listing and installing a published release

The operator can list the published releases of flai and of the dashboard and install any one of them, an earlier one included, from the CLI, the host API, and the Updates page (S-0298). Installing a chosen release goes the way installing the newest does. What counts as published, what the dashboard may name, and why MCP only lists are decided in [ADR-0117](../adrs/0117-the-host-api-installs-a-release-the-dashboard-names-only-when-flai-lists-it-as.md).

| Surface | flai | Dashboard |
|---------|------|-----------|
| List, CLI | `flai self-upgrade --list`, or `flai host versions` through the host | `flai dashboard versions` |
| Install, CLI | `flai self-upgrade --version X.Y.Z`, or `flai host upgrade --version X.Y.Z` through the host | `flai dashboard upgrade --tag X.Y.Z [--published]` |
| Host's control API | `GET /versions`, `POST /upgrade {version?}` | None |
| Host API (the dashboard) | `host.versions`, `host.upgrade {version?}` | `dashboard.versions`, `dashboard.upgrade {tag?}` |
| MCP | `versions`, a read | `versions`, a read |

- **The lists.** `selfupgrade.List` reads the GitHub releases of the configured releases repository (`FLAI_RELEASES_API`, as `self-upgrade` does) tagged `flai/vX.Y.Z`, neither drafts nor prereleases, across every page, newest first by semver. `selfupgrade.ListTags` reads the repository's tags `flaiover/vX.Y.Z` with no suffix, whose image tag is the bare `X.Y.Z`. `flai self-upgrade --list` marks the installed release (the flai `self-upgrade` would replace), the newest, any below the `flai.minimum` of a project `flai serve` serves, and any this flai cannot verify ([Verifying a release before it is installed](#verifying-a-release-before-it-is-installed)). `flai dashboard versions` marks the release the running container's image carries, the configured `dashboard.tag`, and each release with no digest list this flai can verify ([Verifying a dashboard release before it is run](#verifying-a-dashboard-release-before-it-is-run)). With `--json` each prints a list of `version`, `tag`, `published`, `installed`, and `latest`, and flai's adds `below_minimum`, and `unverifiable`, `signature`, and `reason` for a release it cannot verify; the dashboard's has `running` and `configured` in place of `published` and `installed`, and the same three for a release not deployable. `flai host versions` asks the host's `GET /versions`, which runs `flai self-upgrade --list --json` as `GET /check` runs `--check`, and prints the same.
- **A chosen flai.** `flai self-upgrade --version` refuses a version that is not published, naming the published ones, before anything is downloaded. Otherwise it installs as for the newest, the signature and checksum verified as before. `flai host upgrade --version` sends `{version}` to `POST /upgrade`, which passes it to `self-upgrade`; the host then restarts on what was installed and starts its processes again, as it does for the newest. It says what it installed, what that replaced, and that the host restarts on it. A release below a served project's `flai.minimum` is marked and warned about, not refused.
- **A chosen dashboard.** `flai dashboard upgrade --tag` resolves that release, pulls its image by the digest its verified list names, and swaps the container as `upgrade` does: a temporary container from the new image must answer `/_health` before the running one is stopped. `docker stop` returns before Docker has removed the `--rm` container, so `upgrade` and `restart` then wait for the name to be released (`releaseName`: `docker ps --all` filtered on the exact name, 10 tries 500 ms apart, then `docker rm -f`; [I-0094](../issues/I-0094-the-dashboard-upgrade-stops-the-old-container-and-cannot-start-the-new-one-because-the-name-flaiover-is-still-in-use.md)). When the new image still fails to start under the name, `upgrade` starts the previous image again by the image ID the container ran, not its tag, which the pull may have moved, and exits non-zero, saying whether the previous image runs again or the dashboard is down; the record then holds that ID as its image and the previous tag as its reference. `restart` has nothing to fall back to and says the dashboard is down. With `--published`, a tag that is not a published dashboard release is refused, naming the published ones, and so is one with no digest list this flai can verify (`requirePublishedDashboard`), naming why and the deployable ones, before anything is pulled. Without it the resolver refuses the same releases; only `--image` naming another repository, such as a mirror's, runs a tag as given. The tag applies to that container, not the configuration: `dashboard.tag` is not changed. A restart, `flai dashboard restart` or `flai host`'s watch, keeps the release that runs; it starts the image ID the container runs, never its tag, which `flai dashboard check` may have moved to a newer image ([I-0116](../issues/I-0116-flai-dashboard-restart-starts-the-container-s-tag-again-so-a-newer-image-a-check-pulled-under-a-floating-tag-is-started-without-an-upgrade.md)). Every dashboard container carries the label `io.bytepunx.flai.ref` naming the reference it was started for, which `containerInfo` reads before `{{.Config.Image}}`, so status, `versions`, and restart's output still name the reference: for a flaiover release, `ghcr.io/bytepunx/flaiover@sha256:…`. The record keeps that ID beside the reference (`image`), a release's reference by digest, so that `restart` with no container running starts the same release without resolving `dashboard.tag` again; the watch starts the reference when the record has none, as one an older flai wrote, or when the image is no longer local, logging the latter at warn, and records the ID that then runs. A recorded reference to `ghcr.io/bytepunx/flaiover` by tag, as an older flai wrote, is resolved first (`pinnedRecordRef`), by `restart` and by the watch, so that no restart runs the signed repository unverified: the release its tag names runs by digest and is recorded, and a tag the resolver refuses starts nothing. The next `flai dashboard upgrade` without a tag, or `flai dashboard` starting a container after a stop, uses the configured tag again ([ADR-0118](../adrs/0118-a-dashboard-release-chosen-on-the-updates-page-keeps-running-through-restarts.md)). Pinning stays `flai config set dashboard.tag`.
- **From the dashboard.** `host.upgrade` takes an optional `version` and `dashboard.upgrade` an optional `tag`, each a bare `X.Y.Z`. The host API refuses anything else as invalid params before a command runs, passes `dashboard.upgrade`'s tag with `--published` always, and never takes an image name from the request. Both stay behind their host actions (`host`, `dashboard`) and detached from the request, as for the newest. `host.versions` and `dashboard.versions` are reads, not gated.
- **From an agent.** The MCP tool `versions` answers both lists, marking the flai the server runs, the newest of each, and any flai below the project's `flai.minimum`. No MCP tool installs or deploys a release; that is the operator's, from a shell or the Updates page.

### Verifying a release before it is installed

A flai release is installed only when its `checksums.txt` was signed by a key the installer knows (S-0233, [ADR-0070](../adrs/0070-releases-are-signed-with-a-cosign-key-pair-verified-before-they-are-installed.md)). How releases are signed, and what a signature does and does not prove, is in [release-signing.md](release-signing.md#how-a-release-is-verified-before-it-is-installed).

- **The keys.** `buildinfo.ReleaseKeys` reads the release key's public half, `flai/internal/buildinfo/cosign.pub`, embedded in the binary. `selfupgrade.Options.Keys` replaces it in unit tests. A test build may be linked with one more key to trust beside it (`buildinfo.LinkedTestReleaseKeys`), which only `scripts/install-test.sh` sets and no release build does. No flag or environment variable adds a key. Its fingerprint is under [Current key](../../docs/operators/runbooks/release-key.md#current-key).
- **The check.** `selfupgrade.Download` downloads `checksums.txt` and `checksums.txt.sig`, decodes the signature from base64, and checks it with `ecdsa.VerifyASN1` over the file's SHA-256 against each trusted key. Only then does it download the archive and check its SHA-256 against `checksums.txt`. `flai self-upgrade`, with or without `--version`, installs through it. `flai host upgrade` runs `flai self-upgrade` as the host's child, so the host's flai verifies with its own key, and a refusal is its error: nothing is replaced and nothing restarts.
- **The refusals.** A `*selfupgrade.SignatureError` names the release and its kind. `missing`: the release publishes no `checksums.txt.sig` or no `checksums.txt`, as every release before 1.42.0. `bad`: no trusted key verifies it, nor the release's own `cosign.pub`, so the release may have been altered. `unknown-key`: the release's own `cosign.pub` verifies it and no trusted key does, so the release key changed after this flai was built. For `unknown-key`, `withBridge` lists the published releases, newest first, and names the newest whose signature a trusted key verifies, downloading only each one's `checksums.txt` and signature. The refusal tells the operator to install it with `flai self-upgrade --version`, then upgrade again; when none verifies, to use `install.sh` and compare the key's fingerprint.
- **The lists.** `selfupgrade.VerifyRelease` runs the same check without the archive and without the bridge. `flai self-upgrade --list` (`signatureRefusals`) runs it for every published release, eight at a time, and marks each refusal: `not installable: <summary>` in text, `unverifiable`, `signature` (the kind), and `reason` in JSON. A release with no signature costs no download; one with a signature costs its `checksums.txt` and signature, and its `cosign.pub` when no trusted key verifies it, the first time only (the verdict cache, below). A download that fails fails the list, because a release that could not be checked is not one that cannot be verified. `flai host versions` prints the host's list, so it marks what the host's flai cannot verify. `flai self-upgrade --check` checks the release it reports when it is an upgrade, and says when it is not installable.
- **The verdict cache.** Since S-0379 ([I-0142](../issues/I-0142-flai-self-upgrade-list-and-flai-host-versions-download-two-assets-for-every-signed-release-to-mark-the-ones-they-cannot-verify.md)), `--list` and `--check` check through `selfupgrade.Verdicts` (`verdictcache.go`), which keeps each verdict in `<cache_dir>/releases/verdicts.json`. Its key is the release's tag, the API URLs of its `checksums.txt`, `checksums.txt.sig`, and `cosign.pub` (empty when it publishes none), and `KeysFingerprint` of the trusted keys: a replaced asset gets a new URL and a build that trusts other keys a new fingerprint, so either is checked again. A listing downloads only the releases it has no verdict for. It keeps verified, `bad`, and `unknown-key`; never a failed download, nor `missing`, which costs no download to find. A missing, unreadable, or corrupt file reads as empty. The file is saved once after the pass, replaced in one step; a failed save is a warning and the listing goes on. Installing never reads it: `selfupgrade.Download` and `withBridge` verify from the network every time, so ADR-0070's guarantee is unchanged.
- **`install.sh`.** It carries the same public key as a PEM string, which a test in `flai/internal/selfupgrade` keeps equal to `cosign.pub`. It needs `openssl` and refuses to start without it. It downloads `checksums.txt.sig`, decodes it with `openssl base64 -d -A`, verifies it with `openssl dgst -sha256 -verify`, and only then checks the archive's SHA-256. It carries one key, so it tells the operator to get the newest `install.sh` when the signature does not verify.

### Verifying a dashboard release before it is run

A flaiover image is run only when a release's digest list, signed by the release key, names its digest (S-0236, [ADR-0070](../adrs/0070-releases-are-signed-with-a-cosign-key-pair-verified-before-they-are-installed.md)). The list's format is in [release-signing.md](release-signing.md#the-digest-list); what each refusal means to the operator is in [the flai guide](../../docs/users/flai.md#what-the-dashboard-verifies).

- **The release client.** `selfupgrade.Find` finds a published release by tag prefix and version, the newest for `""` or `latest`; `FetchAsset` downloads one asset; `FetchSigned` downloads an asset and its `.sig`, `missing` when either is not published; `VerifySigned` checks the signature against the trusted keys (`TrustedKeys`: `Options.Keys`, else the embedded ones) and tells `unknown-key` from `bad` with the release's own `cosign.pub`, never trusting it. `self-upgrade`'s `checksums.txt` and the digest list go through the same `VerifySigned`. `VerifyBlob` checks a signature already downloaded.
- **The list.** `dashboard.Parse` reads a list in the format, refusing any other; `dashboard.Verify` checks its signature against the keys, then parses it. `List.Reference` is `<repository>@<index digest>`, and `List.Matches` matches a `RepoDigests` entry.
- **The cache.** `dashboard.Cache` keeps each verified list and its signature under `flai serve`'s state, `dashboard-digests/<version>.digests` and `.digests.sig`, mode 0600, written atomically, and verifies it again whenever it reads it. `Get` reads one by version, `All` every one that verifies, newest first, naming those skipped, and `Lookup` the newest that names a digest. The dial-time check (S-0238) matches against `All`, and fetches the container's version's list through the resolver when `Get` has none ([release-signing.md](release-signing.md#flai-measures-the-container-before-it-dials)). Only a list that verified is put there.
- **The resolver.** `dashboard.Resolver.Resolve` takes the repository and `dashboard.tag`: `""` or `latest` is the newest GitHub release tagged `flaiover/vX.Y.Z`, neither a draft nor a prerelease (`selfupgrade.List`), and `X.Y.Z` or `vX.Y.Z` that release. It fetches the list with `FetchList`, checks that it is for that version and names the configured repository, caches it, and answers the release, the list, and the reference by digest. A refusal is a `*dashboard.Refusal`, whose kind is `not-a-version`, `no-release`, `missing`, `bad`, `unknown-key`, `malformed`, `version`, or `repository`, and whose message names the release, what was found, and what to do; nothing is cached for it. When the releases or the list cannot be downloaded, it answers the cached list of the version, or for `latest` the newest cached list of the repository, with `Cached` and why; with none, the failure.
- **The commands.** `chooseDashboardImage` resolves for `ghcr.io/bytepunx/flaiover` (`signedDashboardImage`) and for nothing else: `--build` and another repository run `image:tag`, unsigned. `flai dashboard` pulls and runs the reference; `check` compares the running container's `RepoDigests` (`runningRepoDigests`, never `.Image`) with the list, pulling nothing; `upgrade` resolves before it pulls anything and swaps by digest when no digest of the running image is on the list; for an unsigned image both compare image IDs after a pull, as before. `status` matches the running image's `RepoDigests` against the cached lists (`runningProvenance`), so it shows the release only for a list already verified on this host.
- **The list of releases.** `flai dashboard versions` lists the `flaiover/vX.Y.Z` tags (`selfupgrade.ListTags`) and, for each, `digestListRefusals` runs `FetchList` eight at a time: a tag with no GitHub release is `missing` with no download, and a list that does not parse or is for another version is `bad`. A download that fails fails the list. `upgrade --published` checks the chosen release first and the others only when it is refused, to name the deployable ones.

### Allowing unsigned builds

`dashboard.allow_unsigned` lets a development build of either side connect on purpose (S-0239, [ADR-0070](../adrs/0070-releases-are-signed-with-a-cosign-key-pair-verified-before-they-are-installed.md)). What it admits and why is in [release-signing.md](release-signing.md#the-development-case); what the operator does with it, in [docs/operators](../../docs/operators/index.md#allowing-unsigned-builds-s-0239).

- **The setting.** `config.Dashboard.AllowUnsigned`, the key `dashboard.allow_unsigned`, default `false`, among `config.Keys`. The manifest's `dashboard.allow_unsigned`, a `*bool`, overrides it for one project when set (`manifest.Dashboard.AllowUnsignedOr`).
- **flai serve.** `cmd`'s `hostAllowsUnsigned` reads the configuration at each call and is `serve.Options.AllowUnsigned`; `Options.allowsUnsigned(root)` loads the project's manifest over it. `guard` gives each client the image gate and `channel.Client.AllowUnsigned`, both asked before every dial. The gate dials an `unlisted` image when allowed, still recording it `unlisted`, with `allowed` set (`ImageVerdict.Allowed`, which `sameVerdict` compares), and logs a new verdict once at `info`, `dashboard image is on no verified digest list; dialled as unsigned is allowed`. The client accepts a dashboard whose stamp is `missing` when allowed; `invalid signature` and `wrong component` close with 4403 either way, and the credential proof is made either way. `Client.unsignedSides` keeps `unsigned` in the connection's state: `flai` whenever this flai sent no stamp, allowed or not, since the dashboard's allowance let it through, and `dashboard` when the dashboard sent none and flai's allowance admitted it. The client logs one `warn`, `connection allowed unsigned`, with `component` `channel`, `url`, `project`, and `unsigned`.
- **flai dashboard.** `dashboardSettings.AllowUnsigned` is the project's allowance, or `--build`, or `--allow-unsigned`, which writes nothing to the configuration. `containerArgs` adds `--env FLAIOVER_ALLOW_UNSIGNED=1` for it, and the start prints `unsigned allowed: started with FLAIOVER_ALLOW_UNSIGNED=1, it serves a flai with no release stamp`; `--json` has `allow_unsigned`. The record (`dashboard-container.json`) keeps `allow_unsigned`, so `restart`, `upgrade` (`keepAllowance`), and `flai host`'s watch start the container the same way, adding it when the allowance is in force now and never removing it. A container already running without it is left alone, with `unsigned not allowed: <name> already runs, started without FLAIOVER_ALLOW_UNSIGNED=1, which only a start gives; set dashboard.allow_unsigned and run flai dashboard restart`.
- **The statuses.** `unsignedAllowedLine` words the sides as `unsigned allowed: flai`, `unsigned allowed: dashboard`, or `unsigned allowed: flai and dashboard`, for a connected connection only; a signed pair has none. An `unlisted` verdict with `allowed` set is worded by `imageVerdictLine` as `image: the dashboard runs an unsigned image <image ID> (<RepoDigests> or no RepoDigests); dialled, as unsigned is allowed`, and by `describeServeImage` as `flai serve: dials it as unsigned is allowed: the dashboard runs an unsigned image <image ID> (<RepoDigests> or no RepoDigests)`; both name the image with `unsignedImageOf`.

| Command | Text | `--json` |
|---------|------|----------|
| `flai serve status` | `unsigned allowed: <sides>` under the project | `status.connections.<root>.unsigned` |
| `flai host status` | `<root>: unsigned allowed: <sides>` under the `serve` child | `unsigned`, `{<root>: [sides]}` |
| `flai dashboard status` | `unsigned allowed: <sides>` under the `host flai` line; the container's `unsigned allowed: started with FLAIOVER_ALLOW_UNSIGNED=1, it serves a flai with no release stamp`, left out while this project's flai is connected as a signed pair | `allow_unsigned`, and `host_flai.unsigned` |

### Hosts the dashboard accepts

`dashboard.allowed_hosts` names the hosts, besides the address the dashboard is published on, that it accepts in a browser's `Origin`, so that it can be reached through a tunnel or a domain (S-0489). The dashboard's reading of it is in [flaiover-dashboard.md](flaiover-dashboard.md); the operator sets it with `flai config set dashboard.allowed_hosts`, or on the dashboard's Host page.

- **The setting.** `config.Dashboard.AllowedHosts`, the key `dashboard.allowed_hosts`, a string, empty by default, among `config.Keys`: host names, each with an optional port, separated by commas, such as `flow.example.com, board.example.net:8443`. It is a fact about one machine's shared container, so it is in the configuration only, never the manifest. `config.Parse`, and so `flai config set`, refuses an entry with a scheme, a path, a space, `@`, a port that is not 1 to 65535, or an IPv6 address without brackets, naming each. `config.AllowedHostList` trims each entry, drops the empty ones, and lower-cases them; `config.NormalizeAllowedHosts` joins them with commas.
- **flai dashboard.** `dashboardSettings.AllowedHosts` is the configuration's list, normalized, read afresh by every command, and `containerArgs` adds `--env FLAIOVER_ALLOWED_HOSTS=<list>` when it names any: a comma-separated list, lower-case, with no spaces and no scheme, the contract with the dashboard. The record (`dashboard-container.json`) keeps it as `allowed_hosts`. `restart` and `upgrade`, its temporary container included, start the container with the configuration's list as it is then, not the record's, so a changed setting applies on the next start; `flai host`'s watch does the same, and keeps the record's list only when the configuration cannot be read. A container already running keeps the list it was started with: only a start gives it one.
- **The statuses.** `flai dashboard status` says, for a running container, `allowed hosts: started with FLAIOVER_ALLOWED_HOSTS=<list>` when the record names any, and `allowed hosts: dashboard.allowed_hosts is <list or empty> now; flai dashboard restart applies it` when the configuration names another (`describeAllowedHosts`); `--json` has `allowed_hosts`, the record's list, `""` for none, for a running container. `settings.get`'s host block (`hostSettings`) has `allowed_hosts`, the configuration's list, normalized, so that the Host page can say when the two differ.
- **The Host page.** `settings.dashboard_hosts` (`internal/hostapi`, S-0489) sets the list from the dashboard. It takes `{hosts}`, a string, and runs `flai config set -- dashboard.allowed_hosts <hosts>`, the list normalized; an empty string clears it. It is host-wide, gated by the `settings` action like `settings.dashboard_token`, so it needs `flai serve enable settings --all-projects`. An entry that is not a host name is refused as invalid params before flai runs, with the reason `config.Parse` gives, so the rule lives in one place. A line break or NUL in `hosts`, or no `hosts`, is refused too. flai's own refusal, exit 1, is a rule with flai's reason. The container keeps the list it was started with: the Host page says a restart applies a saved list ([flaiover-dashboard.md](flaiover-dashboard.md), [ADR-0193](../adrs/0193-the-dashboard-accepts-a-browser-origin-naming-one-of-the-hosts-in-dashboard.md)). It is the one method that runs `flai config`, and only for this key.

## Design system

S-0381, [ADR-0146](../adrs/0146-a-project-s-design-tokens-are-a-dtcg-file-the-manifest-names-beside-its.md), [ADR-0148](../adrs/0148-a-design-token-s-colour-is-in-the-srgb-colour-space-with-its-hex-or-in-oklch.md). A project's design system is its token file, the values its UI uses, and its guidance, the words that say when to use which. Why a project keeps one and how the dashboard and agents use it is [design-system-tool.md](design-system-tool.md). The dashboard's Design page is S-0382's. The dashboard reads and saves the token file through `file.get` and `file.save` (S-0436, [ADR-0147](../adrs/0147-the-dashboard-reads-and-saves-a-text-file-that-is-not-markdown-through-file-get.md)), `flai file show` and `flai file save` on the host ([Commands](#commands)).

**Proposals.** S-0384, [ADR-0151](../adrs/0151-the-operator-and-agents-change-a-design-system-by-edits-on-the-design-page.md). A change to the design system is proposed on a story's branch: a proposal is an open story whose branch, or uncommitted worktree, changes the token file, the guidance, or both. `flai design proposals` and `design.proposals` list them, and `flai design show --story` and `design.get` with a story read one from its worktree ([Commands](#commands)). Nothing else holds a proposal. Accepting the story merges its branch, and with it the main checkout's design system becomes the proposal's.

| File | Path | Answered by |
|------|------|-------------|
| Token file | The manifest's `design.tokens`, `design/system/design-system.tokens.json` when unset ([project-manifest.md](project-manifest.md)) | `Manifest.DesignTokens()` |
| Guidance | `design/system/design-system.md`, a design document like any other | `Manifest.DesignGuidance()` |

**Two layouts.** The token file is DTCG 2025.10 JSON, in one of two layouts (the package comment of `flai/internal/designsystem/tokens.go`):

- A plain token file, with no `resolutionOrder`. Each token has one value, which serves light and dark.
- A resolver document, recognised by its top-level `resolutionOrder`, with its sources inline. Its sets' sources give the light values. The dark values sit in `modifiers.theme.contexts.dark`, each an override of a token a set defines, which keeps that token's type. A token dark does not override has its light value in dark too. flai reads one modifier, `theme`, with the contexts `light` and `dark`, and the resolver `version` `2025.10`; any other modifier, context, or property, and a `$ref` source, is a problem. `flai design init` writes this layout.

**The subset flai reads.** Six groups, named after Tailwind v4's theme namespaces, each holding one level of names:

| Group | `$type` | Required tokens |
|-------|---------|-----------------|
| `color` | `color` | The roles of `ColorRoles`: `background`, `surface`, `text`, `text-muted`, `border`, `primary`, `on-primary`, `secondary`, `on-secondary`, `focus`, and `success`, `warning`, `danger`, `info`, each with its `-soft` tint |
| `font` | `fontFamily` | `sans`, `mono` |
| `text` | `typography` or `dimension` | `xs`, `sm`, `base`, `lg`, `xl`, `2xl`, `3xl`, `4xl` |
| `spacing` | `dimension` | One token, the group key itself |
| `radius` | `dimension` | `sm`, `md`, `lg` |
| `shadow` | `shadow` | `sm`, `md`, `lg` |

`RequiredPaths()` lists them. A colour is `srgb` with its six-digit `hex`, which must agree with its components, or `oklch`; a role is opaque, and alpha is for shadows and extra colours only. A dimension is in `rem` or `px`. A name is lower-case letters, digits, and hyphens, so that it can be part of a CSS custom property. An alias, `{color.primary}`, resolves within each theme and takes its target's type. Extra tokens are allowed in the six groups; a token outside them, or of a type its group does not hold, is a problem. `Validate` and the resolver say each problem at its token path and line.

**Contrast.** `Contrast` reports each role pair ADR-0148 names, per theme, as WCAG 2 ratios of the colours as their hex gives them:

| Foreground | Background | Threshold |
|------------|------------|-----------|
| `text`, `text-muted` | `background`, `surface` | 4.5:1 |
| `success`, `warning`, `danger`, `info` | `background`, `surface`, its own `-soft` tint | 4.5:1 |
| `on-primary`, `on-secondary` | `primary`, `secondary` | 4.5:1 |
| `border`, `focus` | `background` | 3:1 |

An `oklch` colour is converted to sRGB through OKLab and clamped to the gamut; a pair that needed it says `clipped`. The ratio is truncated to two decimals, so it never reads as passing when it fails. Contrast is a report: `flai design show` prints it, and it is never a `flai check` finding.

**Renderings.** `CSS` renders every token as CSS custom properties with Tailwind v4's names, such as `--color-primary` and `--text-sm` (a typography step adds its line height, weight, and letter spacing): a `:root` rule for light, then a `[data-theme='dark']` rule for dark, each with every token. `Tailwind` renders `@custom-variant dark (&:where([data-theme='dark'], [data-theme='dark'] *));`, then an `@theme` block of the light values, then a `[data-theme='dark']` rule of the dark values that differ, so the dark values and Tailwind's `dark:` variant follow the same attribute ([ADR-0159](../adrs/0159-the-generated-design-stylesheet-puts-the-dark-values-under-data-theme-dark-and.md)). `Stylesheet` is the generated design stylesheet: a comment header saying it is generated by `flai design css --write` from the token file and is not to be edited, with no date or version, so the same tokens always give the same file, then `Tailwind`'s rendering. `Stale` compares a stylesheet as read with it, CRLF and LF counting as the same.

**The stylesheet.** S-0385, [ADR-0152](../adrs/0152-agents-build-ui-from-a-tailwind-v4-stylesheet-flai-generates-from-the-tokens.md), [ADR-0159](../adrs/0159-the-generated-design-stylesheet-puts-the-dark-values-under-data-theme-dark-and.md). The manifest's `design.stylesheet` names it, a file under a sub-project; unset, there is none ([project-manifest.md](project-manifest.md)). `flai design css --write` writes it, `flai design init` writes it with the token file, and since S-0436 `flai file save` of the token file writes it and commits both in one commit ([ADR-0150](../adrs/0150-a-token-save-from-the-design-page-lands-on-main-and-flai-writes-the-stylesheet.md)), all three through one generator, `docedit.StylesheetFor` ([Commands](#commands)). The UI imports it after `@import "tailwindcss";` and switches theme by setting `data-theme="dark"`. `flai check` reports `design.stale` when it falls behind the tokens and `design.stylesheet` when it lies outside every sub-project.

**Prime.** S-0386, [ADR-0152](../adrs/0152-agents-build-ui-from-a-tailwind-v4-stylesheet-flai-generates-from-the-tokens.md). `flai prime --story` gives the guidance and the token file, and the `design-system.md` convention, to the stories the guidance's `topics` reach, and nothing of it in a project without a guidance document ([agent-context.md § The design system](agent-context.md#the-design-system), `flai prime` in [Commands](#commands)).

**The package.** `flai/internal/designsystem`:

| Function | Does |
|----------|------|
| `Load` | Reads the token file and resolves it; a missing file wraps `fs.ErrNotExist`, and a file that does not parse is a problem, not an error |
| `Resolve` | Turns a parsed file into its light and dark themes, with every problem, a problem both themes give reported once |
| `Validate` | Checks one theme's tokens: groups, names, types, values, and the required tokens |
| `Contrast` | The role pairs above, for one theme |
| `CSS`, `Tailwind` | The renderings above |
| `Stylesheet`, `Stale` | The generated design stylesheet, and whether a stylesheet as read differs from it |
| `Show` | The design system as `flai design show --json` and `design.get` answer it: the paths, the guidance's headings, the layout, each theme's tokens with their CSS, the problems, both renderings, and the contrast |
| `ShowStory` | `Show` on an open story's worktree, at the paths its manifest gives; `Refused` for an item that is not a story, a story done or cancelled, and a story without a worktree (S-0384) |
| `Proposals` | The open stories whose branches, or uncommitted worktrees, change the token file or the guidance, as `Proposal`s in ID order, `[]` for none (S-0384) |
| `StarterTokens`, `StarterGuidance` | The starters `flai design init` writes, embedded by `starter.go`; every role pair passes in both themes |

`flai check` reports each problem as `design.tokens` (`checker.designTokens`), and the stylesheet as `design.stale` and `design.stylesheet` ([Commands](#commands)), `flai design` is the command group ([Commands](#commands)), and `design.get` and `design.proposals` are the host methods (`flai hostapi`).

## Import flow

`flai import` is the deliberate, interactive path for existing repos.

1. Scan the tree. Detect existing `design`, `docs`, `wip`, `adr`, `adrs`, `architecture`, `documentation` folders and any markdown outside code folders. Detect sub-projects by build files (`go.mod`, `package.json`, `pyproject.toml`, `Cargo.toml`).
2. Present a proposal: which folders will be created, which existing folders look like they map to `design`, `docs`, or `wip`, which candidate folders (`adr`, `adrs`, `architecture`, `doc`, `documentation`) move whole into the structure, which loose markdown needs a decision, and which sub-projects were found.
3. Ask for folder names, offering existing folders and the template defaults; `--layout key=name` sets them without asking.
4. Move candidate folders (confirmed one by one in a terminal), then render the template files that do not already exist. Nothing is overwritten; a conflicting file in a moved folder stays where it was and is reported.
5. For each loose markdown file, ask: `design/system`, `design/adrs`, `docs/<audience>`, leave in place, or skip all remaining. Non-interactive runs leave files in place. Moves are `git mv` when the file is tracked.
6. Write `system-flow.yaml` with the detected sub-projects, run `flai check`, print a summary and next steps.
7. Have the host flai serve it (S-0120): when `flai host` runs for the config (`host.Dir.ReadStatus`), register the project with `flai serve` using the entry `flai dashboard` writes (`serveEntry`: key and name from the manifest, the main checkout, `dashboardSettings`' dial address, the shared agent credential, created if missing) and say the dashboard's address; when `flai serve` is stopped under the host, say `flai serve start`. With no host, register nothing and name `flai dashboard` in the project. `--json` carries this as `serve` (`served`, `dashboard`, `reason`, `next`, `root`). A board import runs the same command and `flai serve` then registers it again for the dashboard it was offered on, which replaces the entry by root.

The template's `repo_url` defaults to the `origin` remote made a web address (`webURL`: scp-like, `ssh://`, `git://`, and `http(s)://` remotes become `https://host/path` without credentials, port, or `.git`; a local path or `file://` gives none). `checkRepoURL` refuses a `repo_url` that is not an http or https URL with a host and a path, at the prompt and, for `--var`, before `flai import` or `flai new` changes anything (S-0120).

`--dry-run` prints the proposal and stops (`--json` gives the analysis, the plan, and, since S-0098, the tests `--commit` would run). `--yes` accepts every default. A tree that already has a manifest is refused unless `--force`. With `--json` the proposal's text is no longer printed ahead of the JSON.

`--commit` (S-0098) makes the import one step that ends in a commit:

- Before anything, it refuses a directory that is not a git repository, or has uncommitted changes (`git status --porcelain`), so the commit holds the import and nothing of the operator's.
- After the import it runs tests. It runs the host's `checks.commands` when any are set (`{root}` is the repository; `{story}` is empty). Otherwise `importer.DetectTests` finds the repository's own tests: its own Makefile's `test` target (a Makefile the template brought does not count) stands for everything; otherwise, at the root and in each sub-project, `go test ./...` for `go.mod`, `pnpm`/`yarn`/`npm test` for a `package.json` test script that is not npm's placeholder, `cargo test`, and `python3 -m pytest`. Each runs as an argument list, never through a shell, in turn and all of them, within the checks timeout (15 minutes unless set). Each start and end is logged, which is the progress the board shows.
- When every test passed or none were found, it stages exactly the paths the import wrote and moved (a `git mv` source is already staged) and commits `chore: bring <name> under system-flow (flai import)` with `--trailer` lines.
- When a test fails it commits nothing, leaves the import in the working tree, answers with each test's outcome and the last 8 KiB of its output, and exits 5 (`exitImportNotCommitted`).

## Internal structure

```text
flai/
├── main.go
├── cmd/                 # one file per cobra command
├── internal/
│   ├── config/          # ~/.flai/config.json
│   ├── manifest/        # system-flow.yaml
│   ├── template/        # clone, cache, render
│   ├── workitem/        # parse, validate, transition, ID allocation, board, narratives, archive; parsed items kept per process (S-0156); ordering policies and promotion candidates (S-0217)
│   ├── atomicfile/      # replace a file in one step: a reader sees the old or the new, never half (S-0100)
│   ├── execx/           # git and docker behind a Runner interface
│   ├── logx/            # slog setup per the logging convention: levels incl. fatal, env and flag
│   ├── perf/            # a request's phases, timed beneath its transport; one "request answered" event (S-0152)
│   ├── narrative/       # wip/agents files
│   ├── check/           # reference validator, rule names are stable identifiers
│   ├── mdlint/          # the markdownlint rules what flai writes can break, read from the project's configuration (S-0179, MD007 S-0240, MD038 S-0262, MD056 S-0289), judging what lists, fences, and headings blockquotes hold as it judges them outside one (S-0258)
│   ├── conventions/     # loads and validates design/conventions
│   ├── issues/          # design/issues: record, bump, close, summary
│   ├── components/      # the component library's catalogue: a Svelte sub-project's .svelte files by glob, their props read by the embedded props.mjs with the project's node and svelte/compiler (S-0437); the embedded harness/ (a Vite plugin, its page, and its script), the Vite config written over the sub-project's own, and its own vite started on a loopback port and stopped by process group (S-0438)
│   ├── designsystem/    # the design token file: read, resolve light and dark, validate, contrast, CSS and Tailwind, starters (S-0381); proposals (S-0384)
│   ├── release/         # semver plan from item, commits, and manifest components; tags and version files; pending releases from a history kept per process (S-0157); whether the release policy is met (S-0217)
│   ├── lock/            # system-flow.lock.yaml
│   ├── upgrade/         # classify add, merge, replace, conflict; apply with a policy
│   ├── publish/         # push a local template to its remote: clone, replace, commit, tag, push
│   ├── metrics/         # reference implementation of metrics.md
│   ├── preview/         # what a cancellation, an acceptance, a push, and a publish would do; each --dry-run prints it, flai serve answers with it (S-0159)
│   ├── mergequeue/      # the merge queue: eligibility, its state in .flai-cache/merge-queue and the only reader and writer of it, a batch built and verified, then landed or bisected with its failing story sent back (S-0398, S-0399, S-0400); the event log each write appends to (S-0404)
│   ├── forge/           # the Forge contract pull-request acceptance reaches a forge through, bound to one repository, its token from a TokenFunc at each call; GitHub's adapter over the REST API (S-0392, ADR-0165); a story's pull request title and body from the project's pull request template (S-0393, ADR-0166); a story's pull request as flai serve last read it, kept under .flai-cache/forge/status (S-0394, ADR-0167)
│   ├── storygit/        # story branches read with git: the main branch, a story's branch, its diff (S-0159); a story's sync, out of cmd (S-0269); a story's branch opened in its worktree (S-0274)
│   ├── inbox/           # an agent's inbox and its cursor, for the MCP tool inbox, flai task done, and flai story start (S-0269, S-0274)
│   ├── taskdone/        # flai task done: commit, tell, sync, move, log, touches, check, inbox, one result for the CLI, MCP, and the host channel (S-0269, ADR-0107)
│   ├── storystart/      # flai story start: move, stream, prime, inbox, one result for the CLI, MCP, and the host channel (S-0274)
│   ├── verify/          # flai test's tiers, selected by path and run cheapest first (S-0273); flai verify's steps for a story and its stored report (S-0270, ADR-0110)
│   ├── importer/        # scan, plan, moves with git mv
│   ├── dashboard/       # a flaiover release's signed digest list: parse, verify, cache beside flai serve's state; dashboard.tag resolved to an image by digest (S-0236)
│   ├── host/            # flai host: children, control API, one per machine (S-0106)
│   ├── guard/           # flai guard: refuse a sub-agent's writes, hold the planner and the orchestrator to their roles, and refuse a story's agent wait_for_events while its sub-agents run, as Claude Code PreToolUse, SubagentStart, and SubagentStop hooks (S-0175, ADR-0060, S-0208, S-0218, S-0285, ADR-0092); one Decide on a neutral Call, and one reader per harness, Claude Code's built (S-0353, ADR-0130); Decide asks for a story's agent's protected write, and Hold holds an ask for a harness that calls no permission tool (S-0354)
```
